<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We give two independent constructions of binary words of one length at most <i>d</i> whose ordinary edit-distance metrics require <i>ℓ</i><sub>1</sub> distortion $`\exp(\Omega(\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d}))`$ for every sufficiently large <i>d</i>. We also prove a constant-distortion binary conversion for one prescribed input length. Together with the companion upper embedding theorem, these lower bounds determine the order of logarithmic distortion uniformly over finite alphabets with at least two symbols.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-binary)): There are an absolute constant $c>0$ and an integer $d_0$ such that, for every integer $d\geq d_0$, there exist an integer $1\leq n\leq d$ and a finite set $\mathcal W\subseteq\{0,1\}^n$ with $\|\mathcal W\|\geq2$ satisfying $$c_1(\mathcal W,\operatorname{ED}) \geq \exp\!\left(c\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d}\right).$$
  - Explanation: Establishes an asymptotic obstruction for an entire equal-length binary word metric embedded in the space of absolutely summable sequences.
- **C2** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-tagged)): Let $T,B\geq1$ and $m\geq2$ be integers, and let $\Gamma_1,\ldots,\Gamma_m$ be pairwise disjoint alphabets. For $1\leq a,b\leq T$ and $1\leq i\leq m$, take $X_{a,i},Y_{b,i}\in\Gamma_i^B$, and define $$X=\prod_{a=1}^T(X_{a,1}\cdots X_{a,m}),\qquad Y=\prod_{b=1}^T(Y_{b,1}\cdots Y_{b,m}),\qquad N=TmB.$$ Suppose $d\geq0$ is real and $$\label{eq:local-tagged} \sum_{i=1}^m r_B(X_{a,i},Y_{b,i})\geq d \qquad(1\leq a,b\leq T).$$ With $g=(m-1)B$, one has $$\label{eq:tagged-result} r_N(X,Y)\geq\frac{Tdg}{d+g} \geq\frac T2\min(d,g).$$ In particular, $d\leq g$ implies $r_N(X,Y)\geq Td/2$.
  - Explanation: Converts separation in every pair of tagged rows into whole-word subsequence deficit, allowing matches to cross row boundaries.
- **C3** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-cuts)): Let $S$ be finite and $f:S\to\ell_1$. There are nonnegative finite weights $a_C$, indexed by nonempty proper subsets $C\subset S$, such that $$\\|f(x)-f(y)\\|_1 =\sum_{\varnothing\ne C\subsetneq S} a_C\|\mathbf 1_C(x)-\mathbf 1_C(y)\|.$$ Moreover, $$\sum_{\varnothing\ne C\subsetneq S}a_C \leq\sum_{\{x,y\}\subset S,\ x\ne y}\\|f(x)-f(y)\\|_1,$$ where the sum on the right is over unordered pairs.
  - Explanation: Expresses distances on a finite image as a nonnegative sum of cut separations and controls the total cut weight.
- **C4** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-primes)): There is an absolute $c>0$ such that $\pi(x)\geq cx/\log x$ for all sufficiently large real $x$. Consequently, for all sufficiently large integers $k$, the interval $[k^4,k^5]$ contains at least $20k^2$ primes and $[k^2,k^3]$ contains more than $1600k$ primes.
  - Explanation: Supplies sufficiently many distinct primes for the period and congruence constructions in the stated asymptotic regime.
- **C5** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-delimiter)): Let $\Gamma$ be a finite alphabet of size $A\geq2$, let $t\geq\lceil\log_2A\rceil$ be an integer, and put $B=2t+3$. Give the symbols of $\Gamma$ distinct labels $(a_1,\ldots,a_t)\in\{0,1\}^t$. Let $c(a)$ be the prefix $110$ followed, in order, by the two-bit words $a_j0$ for $j=1,\ldots,t$. Let $\mathcal B$ extend $c$ by concatenation. For every integer $n\geq0$ and every $x,y\in\Gamma^n$, $$\label{eq:delimiter} \frac12r_n(x,y) \leq\operatorname{ED}(\mathcal B(x),\mathcal B(y)) \leq B\operatorname{ED}(x,y).$$
  - Explanation: Provides an explicit concatenation code whose delimiter distinguishes block starts and controls edit distances for equal-length source words.
- **C6** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-orders)): For every node $v$, $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(w_v)=Q_v$. At every internal node, $$\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(u_v)=Q_vR_v,\qquad \gcd(Q_v,R_v)=1.$$
  - Explanation: Determines the vector orders from disjoint subtree supports and distinct prime factors.
- **C7** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-finite-cost)): For every leaf $u$, every $x\in A$ and every integer $t$, $$\operatorname{ED}(G(x),G(x+t e_u))\leq M/m^k.$$ If $v$ is internal at depth $j$, then for every $x\in A$ and integer $1\leq s\leq T$, $$\label{eq:finite-cost} \operatorname{ED}(G(x),G(x+s u_v))\leq\frac{2s}{T}\frac{M}{m^j}.$$
  - Explanation: Bounds the costs of arbitrary leaf-coordinate changes and bounded row advances, uniformly over parameter states.
- **C8** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-finite-separation)): For every node $v$ of height $h$, every $x\in A$, and every integer $t$ with $Q_v\nmid t$, $$\label{eq:finite-separation} r_{L_v}\bigl(G_v(x),G_v(x+t w_v)\bigr)\geq4^{-h}L_v.$$
  - Explanation: Proves a pointwise subsequence deficit for distinguished translations not divisible by the node period.
- **C9** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-finite-fourier)): If $F:A\to\ell_1$ is any map on a finite abelian group, then there are weights $H(\chi)\geq0$ such that, for every $z\in A$, $$\label{eq:finite-fourier} \mathbb E_x\\|F(x+z)-F(x)\\|_1 =\sum_{\chi\in\widehat A}\|1-\chi(z)\|^2H(\chi).$$ The map $F$ need not be injective.
  - Explanation: Represents averaged finite-group displacements using nonnegative character weights without requiring injectivity.
- **C10** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-finite-lower)): For all sufficiently large $k$, the word image $\{G(x):x\in A\}$, with ordinary edit distance, has $\ell_1$ distortion at least $C_1^{-1}(k/4)^k$, for an absolute constant $C_1$.
  - Explanation: Compares sparse-activity detection and leaf costs with distinguished root separation to force large distortion.
- **C11** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-torus-good-separation)): Fix the construction data. If a shift $t$ is good at a node $v$ of height $h$, then for every phase vector $\omega$ on its subtree, $$r_{(mT)^h}\bigl(X_v(\omega),X_v(\omega+t)\bigr) \geq\gamma^h(mT)^h.$$
  - Explanation: Turns recursive goodness into separation at every base phase for fixed construction data.
- **C12** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-good-probability)): For all sufficiently large $k$, every node $v$ and every fixed $t\in\mathbb T$ with $\|t\|_{\mathbb T}\geq\epsilon$ satisfy $$\mathbb P[t\text{ is good at }v]\geq9/10,$$ where probability is over the construction in the subtree below $v$. Consequently, there exists a realization of the entire tree for which $$\label{eq:torus-separation} r_N(X(w),X(w+1/2))\geq\gamma^kN \qquad\text{for every }w\in\mathbb T^{m^k}.$$
  - Explanation: Controls goodness for each fixed eligible shift and obtains a realization whose root half-turn separates every base phase.
- **C13** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-torus-fourier)): Let $n\geq1$ be an integer and let $F:\mathbb T^n\to\ell_1$ be a measurable map with finite range. Set $$L_F(h)=\mathbb E_w\\|F(w+h)-F(w)\\|_1 \qquad(h\in\mathbb T^n),$$ using normalized Haar measure. There are summable coefficients $\mu_\xi\geq0$, indexed by $\xi\in\mathbb Z^n$, such that $$\label{eq:torus-fourier} L_F(h)=\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb Z^n} \mu_\xi\bigl(1-\cos(2\pi\langle\xi,h\rangle)\bigr) \qquad(h\in\mathbb T^n).$$
  - Explanation: Represents averaged displacements of a measurable finite-range torus map by summable nonnegative frequency weights.
- **C14** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-detection)): For the fixed construction and its shifts $h_v(a)$, every measurable finite-range $F:\mathbb T^{m^k}\to\ell_1$ satisfies $$\begin{aligned} L_F(e_o/2)\leq{}& 4\sum_{v\text{ all nodes}} \mathbb E_{\eta\in[0,1/Q]}L_F(\eta e_v) +2\sum_{v\text{ internal}} \mathbb E_{a\in\mathbb Z/P_v\mathbb Z}L_F(h_v(a))\notag\\ &\quad+2k^{-k}\sum_{u\text{ leaf}} \mathbb E_{\eta\in\mathbb T}L_F(\eta e_u). \label{eq:detection} \end{aligned}$$ Every interval, finite-group and circle average has total mass one.
  - Explanation: Bounds the root half-turn by normalized averages of tiny rotations, internal cycle shifts and leaf rotations.
- **C15** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-companion-upper)): There are absolute constants $C>0$ and $d_0\geq3$ such that, for every finite alphabet $\Sigma$ with $\|\Sigma\|\geq2$ and every integer $d\geq d_0$, there are an integer $M\geq1$ and one deterministic map $f:\Sigma^{\leq d}\to\ell_1^M$ satisfying $$\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\leq\\|f(x)-f(y)\\|_1 \leq\exp\!\left(C\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d}\right)\operatorname{ED}(x,y) \qquad(x,y\in\Sigma^{\leq d}).$$ Here $\ell_1^M$ is $\mathbb R^M$ with its usual $\ell_1$ norm. The domain includes the empty word, and $\operatorname{ED}$ is ordinary unit-cost insertion, deletion and substitution distance. The dimension may depend on $\Sigma,d$; the constants and threshold do not.
  - Explanation: States the companion upper embedding with constants uniform over finite alphabets and a domain including the empty word.
- **C16** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-matching)): There are absolute $c,C>0$ and an integer $d_2$ such that for every integer $d\geq d_2$ and every finite alphabet $\Sigma$ with $\|\Sigma\|\geq2$, $$\label{eq:matching} \exp\!\left(c\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d}\right) \leq E_\Sigma(d)\leq \exp\!\left(C\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d}\right).$$ The same bounds hold for $\sup_{\Sigma:\,2\leq\|\Sigma\|<\infty}E_\Sigma(d)$.
  - Explanation: Combines binary lower witnesses with the companion upper estimate to determine the exponential distortion scale uniformly over finite alphabets.
- **C17** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-804)): There is an absolute integer $M_0$ such that, for every integer $M\geq M_0$ and every alphabet $\Gamma$ with $1\leq K=\|\Gamma\|\leq M$, there exists a map $c:\Gamma\to\{0,1\}^{P}$ with $P=M^2$ whose concatenation extension $\operatorname{enc}$ satisfies $$\label{eq:804} \frac P{804}\operatorname{ED}(x,y) \leq\operatorname{ED}(\operatorname{enc}(x),\operatorname{enc}(y))\leq P\operatorname{ED}(x,y) \qquad(x,y\in\Gamma^M).$$ The encoded words have length $MP=M^3$. More precisely, $$\label{eq:402} r_{MP}(\operatorname{enc}(x),\operatorname{enc}(y))\geq\frac P{402}r_M(x,y) \qquad(x,y\in\Gamma^M).$$
  - Explanation: Provides an existence theorem for a binary codebook tied to a prescribed input length; its guarantee holds simultaneously for every pair of that length.
- **C18** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-random-segments)): For sufficiently large integers $M$ and integers $1\leq K\leq M$, there are $K$ binary words of length $P=M^2$ such that any two length-$a$ segments of distinct codewords satisfy $\operatorname{LCS}\leq0.99a$ whenever $$a\geq a_0:=\lceil40\log_2(MP)\rceil =\lceil120\log_2M\rceil.$$
  - Explanation: Uses random bits and a union bound to obtain codewords with separated long segments whenever their labels differ.
- **C19** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-torus-costs)): For the realization fixed after Proposition <a href="#prop:good-probability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:good-probability">13</a>, the following estimates hold, with $q$ denoting the depth of $v$ in the first two statements.* 1. *If $v$ is internal and $a$ is an integer with $0\leq a<P_v$, then for every $w$, $$\label{eq:cycle-cost} \operatorname{ED}(X(w),X(w+h_v(a)))\leq2Nm^{-q}\frac{P_v}{T}.$$* 2. *If $v$ is any node and $0\leq\eta\leq1/Q$, then $$\label{eq:tiny-cost} \mathbb E_w\operatorname{ED}(X(w),X(w+\eta e_v))\leq Nm^{-q}\frac RQ,$$ where $w$ has normalized Haar measure on $\mathbb T^{m^k}$.* 3. *If $u$ is a leaf, then for every $w$ and $\eta\in\mathbb T$, $$\label{eq:leaf-cost} \operatorname{ED}(X(w),X(w+\eta e_u))\leq Nm^{-k}.$$
  - Explanation: Bounds cycle and leaf translations pointwise and tiny rotations in Haar average; the averaged estimate is not asserted for every individual phase.

```json
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-binary",
        "exact_quote": "There are an absolute constant $c>0$ and an integer $d_0$ such that, for every integer $d\\geq d_0$, there exist an integer $1\\leq n\\leq d$ and a finite set $\\mathcal W\\subseteq\\{0,1\\}^n$ with $|\\mathcal W|\\geq2$ satisfying $$c_1(\\mathcal W,\\operatorname{ED})\n \\geq \\exp\\!\\left(c\\sqrt{\\log d\\,\\log\\log d}\\right).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-099.3"
      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-tagged",
        "exact_quote": "Let $T,B\\geq1$ and $m\\geq2$ be integers, and let $\\Gamma_1,\\ldots,\\Gamma_m$ be pairwise disjoint alphabets. For $1\\leq a,b\\leq T$ and $1\\leq i\\leq m$, take $X_{a,i},Y_{b,i}\\in\\Gamma_i^B$, and define $$X=\\prod_{a=1}^T(X_{a,1}\\cdots X_{a,m}),\\qquad\n Y=\\prod_{b=1}^T(Y_{b,1}\\cdots Y_{b,m}),\\qquad N=TmB.$$ Suppose $d\\geq0$ is real and $$\\label{eq:local-tagged}\n \\sum_{i=1}^m r_B(X_{a,i},Y_{b,i})\\geq d\n \\qquad(1\\leq a,b\\leq T).$$ With $g=(m-1)B$, one has $$\\label{eq:tagged-result}\n r_N(X,Y)\\geq\\frac{Tdg}{d+g}\n \\geq\\frac T2\\min(d,g).$$ In particular, $d\\leq g$ implies $r_N(X,Y)\\geq Td/2$.",
        "source_id": "PM-099.3"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-tagged",
          "exact_quote": "Let $T,B\\geq1$ and $m\\geq2$ be integers, and let $\\Gamma_1,\\ldots,\\Gamma_m$ be pairwise disjoint alphabets.           "source_id": "PM-099.3"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $T,B\\geq1$ and $m\\geq2$ be integers, and let $\\Gamma_1,\\ldots,\\Gamma_m$ be pairwise disjoint alphabets.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-cuts",
        "exact_quote": "Let $S$ be finite and $f:S\\to\\ell_1$. There are nonnegative finite weights $a_C$, indexed by nonempty proper subsets $C\\subset S$, such that $$\\|f(x)-f(y)\\|_1\n =\\sum_{\\varnothing\\ne C\\subsetneq S}\n a_C|\\mathbf 1_C(x)-\\mathbf 1_C(y)|.$$ Moreover, $$\\sum_{\\varnothing\\ne C\\subsetneq S}a_C\n \\leq\\sum_{\\{x,y\\}\\subset S,\\ x\\ne y}\\|f(x)-f(y)\\|_1,$$ where the sum on the right is over unordered pairs.",
        "source_id": "PM-099.3"
      },
      "evidence": [

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-primes",
        "exact_quote": "There is an absolute $c>0$ such that $\\pi(x)\\geq cx/\\log x$ for all sufficiently large real $x$. Consequently, for all sufficiently large integers $k$, the interval $[k^4,k^5]$ contains at least $20k^2$ primes and $[k^2,k^3]$ contains more than $1600k$ primes.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-delimiter",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\Gamma$ be a finite alphabet of size $A\\geq2$, let $t\\geq\\lceil\\log_2A\\rceil$ be an integer, and put $B=2t+3$. Give the symbols of $\\Gamma$ distinct labels $(a_1,\\ldots,a_t)\\in\\{0,1\\}^t$. Let $c(a)$ be the prefix $110$ followed, in order, by the two-bit words $a_j0$ for $j=1,\\ldots,t$. Let $\\mathcal B$ extend $c$ by concatenation. For every integer $n\\geq0$ and every $x,y\\in\\Gamma^n$, $$\\label{eq:delimiter}\n \\frac12r_n(x,y)\n \\leq\\operatorname{ED}(\\mathcal B(x),\\mathcal B(y))\n \\leq B\\operatorname{ED}(x,y).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-099.3"
      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-finite-fourier",
        "exact_quote": "If $F:A\\to\\ell_1$ is any map on a finite abelian group, then there are weights $H(\\chi)\\geq0$ such that, for every $z\\in A$, $$\\label{eq:finite-fourier}\n \\mathbb E_x\\|F(x+z)-F(x)\\|_1\n =\\sum_{\\chi\\in\\widehat A}|1-\\chi(z)|^2H(\\chi).$$ The map $F$ need not be injective.",
        "source_id": "PM-099.3"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-finite-fourier",
          "exact_quote": "If $F:A\\to\\ell_1$ is any map on a finite abelian group, then there are weights $H(\\chi)\\geq0$ such that, for every $z\\in A$, $$\\label{eq:finite-fourier}\n \\mathbb E_x\\|F(x+z)-F(x)\\|_1\n =\\sum_{\\chi\\in\\widehat A}|1-\\chi(z)|^2H(\\chi).$$ The map $F$ need not be injective.",
          "source_id": "PM-099.3"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "If $F:A\\to\\ell_1$ is any map on a finite abelian group, then there are weights $H(\\chi)\\geq0$ such that, for every $z\\in A$, $$\\label{eq:finite-fourier}\n \\mathbb E_x\\|F(x+z)-F(x)\\|_1\n =\\sum_{\\chi\\in\\widehat A}|1-\\chi(z)|^2H(\\chi).$$ The map $F$ need not be injective.",
      "explanation": "Represents averaged finite-group displacements using nonnegative character weights without requiring injectivity.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-finite-fourier",
          "exact_quote": "If $F:A\\to\\ell_1$ is any map on a finite abelian group, then there are weights $H(\\chi)\\geq0$ such that, for every $z\\in A$, $$\\label{eq:finite-fourier}\n \\mathbb E_x\\|F(x+z)-F(x)\\|_1\n =\\sum_{\\chi\\in\\widehat A}|1-\\chi(z)|^2H(\\chi).$$ The map $F$ need not be injective.",
          "source_id": "PM-099.3"
        }
      ],

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-torus-fourier",
        "exact_quote": "Let $n\\geq1$ be an integer and let $F:\\mathbb T^n\\to\\ell_1$ be a measurable map with finite range. Set $$L_F(h)=\\mathbb E_w\\|F(w+h)-F(w)\\|_1\n \\qquad(h\\in\\mathbb T^n),$$ using normalized Haar measure. There are summable coefficients $\\mu_\\xi\\geq0$, indexed by $\\xi\\in\\mathbb Z^n$, such that $$\\label{eq:torus-fourier}\n L_F(h)=\\sum_{\\xi\\in\\mathbb Z^n}\n \\mu_\\xi\\bigl(1-\\cos(2\\pi\\langle\\xi,h\\rangle)\\bigr)\n \\qquad(h\\in\\mathbb T^n).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-099.3"
      },

          "source_id": "PM-099.3"
        }

        "exact_quote": "For the fixed construction and its shifts $h_v(a)$, every measurable finite-range $F:\\mathbb T^{m^k}\\to\\ell_1$ satisfies $$\\begin{aligned}\n L_F(e_o/2)\\leq{}&\n 4\\sum_{v\\text{ all nodes}}\n \\mathbb E_{\\eta\\in[0,1/Q]}L_F(\\eta e_v)\n +2\\sum_{v\\text{ internal}}\n \\mathbb E_{a\\in\\mathbb Z/P_v\\mathbb Z}L_F(h_v(a))\\notag\\\\\n &\\quad+2k^{-k}\\sum_{u\\text{ leaf}}\n \\mathbb E_{\\eta\\in\\mathbb T}L_F(\\eta e_u).\n \\label{eq:detection}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Every interval, finite-group and circle average has total mass one.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-companion-upper",
        "exact_quote": "There are absolute constants $C>0$ and $d_0\\geq3$ such that, for every finite alphabet $\\Sigma$ with $|\\Sigma|\\geq2$ and every integer $d\\geq d_0$, there are an integer $M\\geq1$ and one deterministic map $f:\\Sigma^{\\leq d}\\to\\ell_1^M$ satisfying $$\\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\\leq\\|f(x)-f(y)\\|_1\n \\leq\\exp\\!\\left(C\\sqrt{\\log d\\,\\log\\log d}\\right)\\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\n \\qquad(x,y\\in\\Sigma^{\\leq d}).$$ Here $\\ell_1^M$ is $\\mathbb R^M$ with its usual $\\ell_1$ norm. The domain includes the empty word, and $\\operatorname{ED}$ is ordinary unit-cost insertion, deletion and substitution distance. The dimension may depend on $\\Sigma,d$; the constants and threshold do not.",
        "source_id": "PM-099.3"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-thm-companion-upper",
          "exact_quote": "There are absolute constants $C>0$ and $d_0\\geq3$ such that, for every finite alphabet $\\Sigma$ with $|\\Sigma|\\geq2$ and every integer $d\\geq d_0$, there are an integer $M\\geq1$ and one deterministic map $f:\\Sigma^{\\leq d}\\to\\ell_1^M$ satisfying $$\\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\\leq\\|f(x)-f(y)\\|_1\n \\leq\\exp\\!\\left(C\\sqrt{\\log d\\,\\log\\log d}\\right)\\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\n \\qquad(x,y\\in\\Sigma^{\\leq d}).$$ Here $\\ell_1^M$ is $\\mathbb R^M$ with its usual $\\ell_1$ norm. The domain includes the empty word, and $\\operatorname{ED}$ is ordinary unit-cost insertion, deletion and substitution distance. The dimension may depend on $\\Sigma,d$; the constants and threshold do not.",
          "source_id": "PM-099.3"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "There are absolute constants $C>0$ and $d_0\\geq3$ such that, for every finite alphabet $\\Sigma$ with $|\\Sigma|\\geq2$ and every integer $d\\geq d_0$, there are an integer $M\\geq1$ and one deterministic map $f:\\Sigma^{\\leq d}\\to\\ell_1^M$ satisfying $$\\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\\leq\\|f(x)-f(y)\\|_1\n \\leq\\exp\\!\\left(C\\sqrt{\\log d\\,\\log\\log d}\\right)\\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\n \\qquad(x,y\\in\\Sigma^{\\leq d}).$$ Here $\\ell_1^M$ is $\\mathbb R^M$ with its usual $\\ell_1$ norm.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cor-matching",
        "exact_quote": "There are absolute $c,C>0$ and an integer $d_2$ such that for every integer $d\\geq d_2$ and every finite alphabet $\\Sigma$ with $|\\Sigma|\\geq2$, $$\\label{eq:matching}\n \\exp\\!\\left(c\\sqrt{\\log d\\,\\log\\log d}\\right)\n \\leq E_\\Sigma(d)\\leq\n \\exp\\!\\left(C\\sqrt{\\log d\\,\\log\\log d}\\right).$$ The same bounds hold for $\\sup_{\\Sigma:\\,2\\leq|\\Sigma|<\\infty}E_\\Sigma(d)$.",

      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-cor-matching",
          "exact_quote": "There are absolute $c,C>0$ and an integer $d_2$ such that for every integer $d\\geq d_2$ and every finite alphabet $\\Sigma$ with $|\\Sigma|\\geq2$, $$\\label{eq:matching}\n \\exp\\!\\left(c\\sqrt{\\log d\\,\\log\\log d}\\right)\n \\leq E_\\Sigma(d)\\leq\n \\exp\\!\\left(C\\sqrt{\\log d\\,\\log\\log d}\\right).$$ The same bounds hold for $\\sup_{\\Sigma:\\,2\\leq|\\Sigma|<\\infty}E_\\Sigma(d)$.",
          "source_id": "PM-099.3"
        }

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-804",
        "exact_quote": "There is an absolute integer $M_0$ such that, for every integer $M\\geq M_0$ and every alphabet $\\Gamma$ with $1\\leq K=|\\Gamma|\\leq M$, there exists a map $c:\\Gamma\\to\\{0,1\\}^{P}$ with $P=M^2$ whose concatenation extension $\\operatorname{enc}$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:804}\n \\frac P{804}\\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\n \\leq\\operatorname{ED}(\\operatorname{enc}(x),\\operatorname{enc}(y))\\leq P\\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\n \\qquad(x,y\\in\\Gamma^M).$$ The encoded words have length $MP=M^3$. More precisely, $$\\label{eq:402}\n r_{MP}(\\operatorname{enc}(x),\\operatorname{enc}(y))\\geq\\frac P{402}r_M(x,y)\n \\qquad(x,y\\in\\Gamma^M).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-099.3"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-thm-804",
          "exact_quote": "There is an absolute integer $M_0$ such that, for every integer $M\\geq M_0$ and every alphabet $\\Gamma$ with $1\\leq K=|\\Gamma|\\leq M$, there exists a map $c:\\Gamma\\to\\{0,1\\}^{P}$ with $P=M^2$ whose concatenation extension $\\operatorname{enc}$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:804}\n \\frac P{804}\\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\n \\leq\\operatorname{ED}(\\operatorname{enc}(x),\\operatorname{enc}(y))\\leq P\\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\n \\qquad(x,y\\in\\Gamma^M).$$ The encoded words have length $MP=M^3$. More precisely, $$\\label{eq:402}\n r_{MP}(\\operatorname{enc}(x),\\operatorname{enc}(y))\\geq\\frac P{402}r_M(x,y)\n \\qquad(x,y\\in\\Gamma^M).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-099.3"
```

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **ordinary edit distance** ([source](#sec:introduction)): An insertion or deletion changes the positions of every later symbol in a word. A long interval can therefore move at small edit cost even when many positions change. This tension between order and inexpensive shifts is central to the question studied here: how faithfully can one represent the edit distances of an entire set of words by distances in $\ell_1$? We use ordinary edit distance $\operatorname{ED}(x,y)$: the minimum number of single-symbol insertions, deletions and substitutions taking $x$ to $y$, each with cost one.
- **length-capped word domain** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a finite alphabet $\Sigma$ with at least two symbols and an integer $d\geq1$, let $\Sigma^{\leq d}$ be the set of words of length at most $d$, including the empty word.
- **embedding distortion** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a finite metric space $(X,\rho)$ with at least two points and an injective map $f:X\to\ell_1$, where $\ell_1$ is the space of absolutely summable real sequences, define $$\operatorname{dist}(f)= \left(\max_{x\ne y}\frac{\\|f(x)-f(y)\\|_1}{\rho(x,y)}\right) \left(\max_{x\ne y}\frac{\rho(x,y)}{\\|f(x)-f(y)\\|_1}\right).$$
- **optimal edit distortion** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Let $c_1(X,\rho)$ be the infimum of $\operatorname{dist}(f)$ over such maps, and put $E_\Sigma(d)=c_1(\Sigma^{\leq d},\operatorname{ED})$.
- **insertion–deletion distance** ([source](#eq:deficit)): The insertion–deletion distance is $$\Delta(x,y)=\|x\|+\|y\|-2\operatorname{LCS}(x,y).$$
- **equal-length subsequence deficit** ([source](#eq:deficit)): For words of equal length $n$, put $r_n(x,y)=n-\operatorname{LCS}(x,y)$.
- **circle and circle distance** ([source](#pm-h-16)): Let $\mathbb T=\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$, with $\|t\|_{\mathbb T}$ denoting the circle distance from $t$ to zero.
- **good shift** ([source](#stmt-def-good)): At a leaf, $t\in\mathbb T$ is good if $\|t\|_{\mathbb T}\geq\epsilon$. At an internal node $v$, it is good if, for every integer $a$ modulo $P_v$, at least $\delta m$ children $vi$ have $t+a c_{vi}/P_v$ good at $vi$.

|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | binary witness distortion | ≥ | $\exp\!\left(c\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d}\right)$ | Every sufficiently large integer cap; absolute constant and threshold. |
| Q2 | tagged-row deficit | ≥ | $\frac{Tdg}{d+g}$ | Every pair of source and target rows satisfies the stated local deficit bound. |
| Q3 | delimiter encoded edit distance lower bound | ≥ | $\frac12r_n(x,y)$ | All equal-length source word pairs with distinct labels of the specified width. |
| Q4 | delimiter encoded edit distance upper bound | ≤ | $B\operatorname{ED}(x,y)$ | All equal-length source word pairs with distinct labels of the specified width. |
| Q5 | finite branching factor | = | $20k$ | Sufficiently large integer tree depth. |
| Q6 | finite row count | = | $k^{20k}$ | Sufficiently large integer tree depth. |
| Q7 | finite distinguished-shift deficit | ≥ | $4^{-h}L_v$ | Every state and every translation multiplier not divisible by the node period. |
| Q8 | finite word-image distortion | ≥ | $C_1^{-1}(k/4)^k$ | Sufficiently large depth and an absolute constant. |
| Q9 | torus group count | = | $16k$ | Sufficiently large integer tree depth. |
| Q10 | torus branching factor | = | $16k^3$ | Sufficiently large integer tree depth. |
| Q11 | torus quantization count | = | $k^{8k}$ | Sufficiently large integer tree depth. |
| Q12 | torus row count | = | $k^{52k}$ | Sufficiently large integer tree depth. |

| Q14 | per-height separation factor | = | $1/128$ | Fixed construction with the stated quantization count. |
| Q15 | fixed-shift goodness probability | ≥ | $9/10$ | Each fixed eligible shift at each node, for sufficiently large depth. |
| Q16 | fixed-length encoded edit lower bound | ≥ | $\frac P{804}\operatorname{ED}(x,y)$ | All source pairs of the prescribed sufficiently large length; alphabet size at most that length. |
| Q17 | fixed-length encoded deficit lower bound | ≥ | $\frac P{402}r_M(x,y)$ | All source pairs of the prescribed sufficiently large length. |
| Q18 | unequal-label segment common subsequence | ≤ | $0.99a$ | Segments at least as long as the stated threshold, with sufficiently large prescribed word length. |

For $1\\leq a,b\\leq T$ and $1\\leq i\\leq m$, take $X_{a,i},Y_{b,i}\\in\\Gamma_i^B$, and define $$X=\\prod_{a=1}^T(X_{a,1}\\cdots X_{a,m}),\\qquad\n Y=\\prod_{b=1}^T(Y_{b,1}\\cdots Y_{b,m}),\\qquad N=TmB.$$ Suppose $d\\geq0$ is real and $$\\label{eq:local-tagged}\n \\sum_{i=1}^m r_B(X_{a,i},Y_{b,i})\\geq d\n \\qquad(1\\leq a,b\\leq T).$$ With $g=(m-1)B$, one has $$\\label{eq:tagged-result}\n r_N(X,Y)\\geq\\frac{Tdg}{d+g}\n \\geq\\frac T2\\min(d,g).$$ In particular, $d\\leq g$ implies $r_N(X,Y)\\geq Td/2$.",
```json
          "source_id": "PM-099.3"
        }
      ],
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "id": "Q2",
      "kind": "bound",

          "exact_quote": "Let $\\Gamma$ be a finite alphabet of size $A\\geq2$, let $t\\geq\\lceil\\log_2A\\rceil$ be an integer, and put $B=2t+3$. Give the symbols of $\\Gamma$ distinct labels $(a_1,\\ldots,a_t)\\in\\{0,1\\}^t$. Let $c(a)$ be the prefix $110$ followed, in order, by the two-bit words $a_j0$ for $j=1,\\ldots,t$. Let $\\mathcal B$ extend $c$ by concatenation. For every integer $n\\geq0$ and every $x,y\\in\\Gamma^n$, $$\\label{eq:delimiter}\n \\frac12r_n(x,y)\n \\leq\\operatorname{ED}(\\mathcal B(x),\\mathcal B(y))\n \\leq B\\operatorname{ED}(x,y).$$",

        }

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-good-probability",
          "exact_quote": "For all sufficiently large $k$, every node $v$ and every fixed $t\\in\\mathbb T$ with $|t|_{\\mathbb T}\\geq\\epsilon$ satisfy $$\\mathbb P[t\\text{ is good at }v]\\geq9/10,$$ where probability is over the construction in the subtree below $v$. Consequently, there exists a realization of the entire tree for which $$\\label{eq:torus-separation}\n r_N(X(w),X(w+1/2))\\geq\\gamma^kN\n \\qquad\\text{for every }w\\in\\mathbb T^{m^k}.$$",
          "source_id": "PM-099.3"
        }
      ],
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "id": "Q15",
      "kind": "bound",
      "prefactor": null,
      "quantity": "fixed-shift goodness probability",
      "regime": {

            "anchor": "stmt-prop-good-probability",
            "exact_quote": "For all sufficiently large $k$, every node $v$ and every fixed $t\\in\\mathbb T$ with $|t|_{\\mathbb T}\\geq\\epsilon$ satisfy $$\\mathbb P[t\\text{ is good at }v]\\geq9/10,$$ where probability is over the construction in the subtree below $v$. Consequently, there exists a realization of the entire tree for which $$\\label{eq:torus-separation}\n r_N(X(w),X(w+1/2))\\geq\\gamma^kN\n \\qquad\\text{for every }w\\in\\mathbb T^{m^k}.$$",
            "source_id": "PM-099.3"
          }
        ],
        "parameter_scope": "Each fixed eligible shift at each node, for sufficiently large depth.",
        "quantifiers": [
          {
```

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-lem-tagged) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-tagged): Charge nonsplit source rows and disjoint unmatched target gaps from split rows. -> Whole-word deficit without aligned row boundaries.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-delimiter) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-delimiter): Recover an original common subsequence from intact delimiter blocks. -> Binary transfer with width-dependent distortion loss.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-prop-finite-separation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-finite-separation): Use distinct prime periods and inductive tagged-row charging. -> Uniform separation of distinguished finite translations.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-prop-finite-lower) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-finite-lower): Apply cut Fourier weights, detect sparse internal activity and count active leaves. -> Finite tree distortion obstruction.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-thm-binary) / [proof](#pm-h-14): Encode the finite witness and compare successive explicit length caps. -> Equal-length binary witnesses below every sufficiently large cap.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-lem-torus-good-separation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-torus-good-separation): Propagate goodness through every row comparison using tagged alphabets. -> Separation for every base phase.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-prop-good-probability) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-good-probability): Separate small and large active prime-group sets, then apply concentration and a union bound. -> Existence of a realization with a good root half-turn.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-lem-detection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-detection): Test large frequency sums and failed congruences; force branching when both tests fail. -> A normalized-average detection inequality.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-thm-binary) / [proof](#pm-h-23): Compare half-turn separation with the detection costs after delimiter encoding and fit the cap. -> Independent binary distortion obstruction.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-cor-matching) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-matching): Project ambient alphabet symbols onto binary symbols and restrict embeddings to the witness. -> Uniform two-sided exponential scale over finite alphabets.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-lem-random-segments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-random-segments): Count increasing matchings of independent binary segments and union-bound forbidden comparisons. -> Existence of a separated codebook.
- **R12** [claim](#stmt-thm-804) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-random-segments-2): Charge unequal-letter matches and decompose equal-letter matches by bipartite edge coloring. -> Constant-distortion transfer for the prescribed input length.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **delimiter_transfer**: The delimiter encoding preserves the source subsequence deficit from below and expands ordinary edit distance by at most the block width.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **constant_width_loss**: Treat the short delimiter code as an absolute constant-distortion alphabet conversion. -> Its distortion loss grows with the block width.
- **global_fixed_length_code**: Use the separate constant-distortion codebook as a guarantee for all input lengths. -> The codebook choice and its guarantee are tied to the prescribed input length.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The delimiter transfer gives an explicit finite binary encoding. The executable covers this construction; it does not implement the asymptotic tree obstruction or the existential fixed-length codebook.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON integers A, t, n.
# PRECONDITIONS: 2 <= A <= 4; 1 <= t <= 3; A <= 2**t; 0 <= n <= 2.
# OUTPUTS: All source words, their delimiter encodings and both exact edit-distance matrices.
# TIME: O(A**(2*n) * (n*(2*t+3)+1)**2).
# SPACE: O(A**(2*n) + (n*(2*t+3)+1)**2).
# ARITHMETIC: Exact integers; no floating point.
# FAILURE: Invalid contract raises ValueError.
import json, sys
from itertools import product
from flint import fmpz
cfg=json.load(sys.stdin)
A,t,n=(cfg[key] for key in ('A','t','n'))
if any(type(q) is not int for q in (A,t,n)) or not (2<=A<=4 and 1<=t<=3 and A<=2**t and 0<=n<=2):
    raise ValueError('outside finite harness contract')
words=list(product(range(A),repeat=n))
def block(a):
    return '110'+''.join(str((a >> j)&1)+'0' for j in range(t-1,-1,-1))
encoded=[''.join(block(a) for a in w) for w in words]
def distance(x,y):
    row=list(range(len(y)+1))
    for i,a in enumerate(x,1):
        nxt=[i]
        for j,b in enumerate(y,1):
            cost=int(fmpz(a != b))
            nxt.append(min(row[j]+1,nxt[-1]+1,row[j-1]+cost))
        row=nxt
    return row[-1]
print(json.dumps({'words':words,'encoded':encoded,'source_ed':[[distance(x,y) for y in words] for x in words], 'encoded_ed':[[distance(x,y) for y in encoded] for x in encoded]},sort_keys=True,separators=(',',':')))
```

width=parameters['t']; length=parameters['n']\n    if any(type(v) is not int for v in (alphabet,width,length)) or not (2<=alphabet<=4 and 1<=width<=3 and alphabet<=2**width and 0<=length<=2):\n        raise ValueError('invalid inputs')\n    originals=[tuple(v) for v in combinatorics.product(range(alphabet),repeat=length)]\n    dictionary={letter:'110'+''.join(digit+'0' for digit in format(letter,'0%db'%width)) for letter in range(alphabet)}\n    binaries=[''.join(dictionary[letter] for letter in seq) for seq in originals]\n    @lru_cache(None)\n    def edit(left,right):\n        if not left: return len(right)\n        if not right: return len(left)\n        return min(1+edit(left[1:],right),1+edit(left,right[1:]),int(left[0]!=right[0])+edit(left[1:],right[1:]))\n    def subsequences(seq):\n        return {tuple(seq[j] for j in range(len(seq)) if mask&(1<<j)) for mask in range(1<<len(seq))}\n    original_subs=[subsequences(s) for s in originals]\n    binary_subs=[subsequences(s) for s in binaries]\n    original_matrix=[]; binary_row.append(dst)\n        original_matrix.append(original_row); binary_matrix.append(binary_row)\n    return dict(words=[list(v) for v in originals],encoded=binaries,source_ed=original_matrix,encoded_ed=binary_matrix)\n\nif __name__=='__main__':\n    try:\n        parameters=js.load(open(system.argv[1]))\n        independently=compute(parameters)\n        offered=js.load(open(system.argv[2]))\n        declared=js.loads(system.argv[3])\n        system.exit(0 if offered==independently==declared else 6)\n    except Exception:\n        system.exit(7)\n",

| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: a distributed-system contract. The word-metric constructions contain no communication, consistency or failure model. |

| other (coding_theory) | True | strong | adaptable | stated | The delimiter code gives an explicit alphabet reduction, while the stronger binary conversion uses a codebook chosen for a prescribed input length. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L-asymptotic** (hypothesis): The binary lower bound requires a sufficiently large length cap; its threshold and absolute constant are unspecified. Blocked use: ['A numerical distortion guarantee for a small specified cap.']
- **L-metric** (formal_scope): The metric assigns unit cost to single-symbol insertion, deletion and substitution, and edit scripts may use intermediate words outside the length cap. Blocked use: ['Weighted edit costs or substitution-only distance.', 'Restricting intermediate edit scripts to the domain length cap.']
- **L-embedding** (counterexample_scope): An embedding represents all pairs in one domain; the obstruction does not rule out efficient estimation of a supplied pair. Blocked use: ['Using embedding distortion as a lower bound on the running time of pairwise distance estimation.']
- **L-computation** (unknown): ABSENT: a runtime, memory or image-dimension bound for an implementation of the embedding results. Blocked use: ['Production resource sizing based only on distortion.']
- **L-matching-scale** (approximation): The uniform comparison determines logarithmic distortion order and leaves constants within the exponent unspecified. Blocked use: ['Claiming a constant-factor estimate for the distortion itself.']
- **L-companion** (formal_scope): The matching upper estimate uses the companion uniform upper embedding theorem; the lower constructions do not require that companion input. Blocked use: ['Describing the upper estimate as proved by either lower construction.']
- **L-conversion-length** (hypothesis): The constant-distortion binary conversion is tied to one prescribed input length and does not assert logarithmic block width. Blocked use: ['Applying its guarantee to arbitrary input lengths with one unchanged codebook.', 'Claiming logarithmic block width for this conversion.']
- **L-conversion-existence** (nonconstructive): The segment-separated codebook is obtained by an existence argument, without a stated efficient codebook construction. Blocked use: ['Treating the random-code existence proof as an efficient deterministic codebook generator.']
- **L-fixed-shift** (formal_scope): The probabilistic goodness statement concerns a fixed eligible shift; separation at every base phase follows after fixing a good realization. Blocked use: ['Claiming one random realization simultaneously makes all eligible shifts good.']
- **L-averaged-cost** (formal_scope): Tiny-rotation edit cost is averaged over the base phase and supplies no pointwise bound for every phase. Blocked use: ['Replacing the Haar-average tiny-rotation cost by a pointwise guarantee.']
- **L-alignment-tags** (hypothesis): The row alignment argument uses equal block lengths, fixed child order and disjoint tags; it does not require row boundaries to match. Blocked use: ['Dropping the tag, order or equal-length assumptions while retaining the row-gap estimate.']
- **L-frequency-cutoff** (hypothesis): Prime divisibility forces branching only after the frequency summaries are below the cutoff. Blocked use: ['Applying the branching conclusion to unrestricted nonzero frequency summaries.']

>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization determines the exponential scale of the least $\ell_1$ distortion of unit-cost edit distance on strings of length at most $d$. For every sufficiently large $d$, uniformly over finite alphabets with at least two symbols, the distortion lies between $\exp(c\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d})$ and $\exp(C\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d})$ for absolute constants $c,C>0$. The lower bound has a witness consisting of binary strings of one common length, and the same scale controls the supremum over finite alphabets.
>
> The formalization gives two finite-circle constructions of binary strings whose least $\ell_1$ distortion is at least $\exp(c\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d})$ for all sufficiently large length caps $d$. The witnesses use strings of one common length and include the binary coding transfers with their stated uniform bounds.
>
> It also gives a finite histogram embedding with distortion at most $\exp(C\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d})$ for all strings of length at most $d$, uniformly over finite alphabets, including the empty string. Thus the lower and upper exponential scales agree up to absolute constants.
>
> The formalization gives two lower-bound constructions for the $\ell_1$ distortion of ordinary edit distance on binary words. For every sufficiently large length cap $d$, each construction supplies a finite set of at least two binary words of one common length at most $d$ whose least $\ell_1$ distortion is at least $\exp(c\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d})$ for an absolute $c>0$.
>
> The selected statements are the binary lower bounds. The paper's constant-distortion binary conversion and the companion upper embedding theorem are outside them.

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace TreeEdit

universe u

/-- A single unit-cost insertion, deletion or substitution, at any position. -/
inductive EditStep {α : Type u} : List α → List α → Prop
  | insert (p q : List α) (a : α) : EditStep (p ++ q) (p ++ a :: q)
  | delete (p q : List α) (a : α) : EditStep (p ++ a :: q) (p ++ q)
  | substitute (p q : List α) (a b : α) : EditStep (p ++ a :: q) (p ++ b :: q)

/-- A script of precisely `n` edits. There is no restriction on its intermediate lengths. -/
inductive EditScript {α : Type u} : List α → List α → ℕ → Prop
  | nil (x : List α) : EditScript x x 0
  | cons {x y z : List α} {n : ℕ} :
      EditStep x y → EditScript y z n → EditScript x z (n + 1)

/-- Ordinary unit-cost edit distance. The set is nonempty (delete then insert). -/
noncomputable def ed {α : Type u} (x y : List α) : ℕ :=
  sInf {n | EditScript x y n}

/-- The full infinite-dimensional real ℓ₁ space. -/
abbrev RealL1 := lp (fun _ : ℕ => ℝ) 1

/-- Product of the two maximal pairwise distance ratios. -/
noncomputable def distortion (W : Finset (List Bool)) (f : W → RealL1) : ℝ :=
  sSup {r : ℝ | ∃ x y : W, x ≠ y ∧
      r = dist (f x) (f y) / (ed x.val y.val : ℝ)} *
  sSup {r : ℝ | ∃ x y : W, x ≠ y ∧
      r = (ed x.val y.val : ℝ) / dist (f x) (f y)}

/-- Infimum over all injective ℓ₁ maps, used on finite sets with at least two points. -/
noncomputable def c1 (W : Finset (List Bool)) : ℝ :=
  sInf {D : ℝ | ∃ f : W → RealL1, Function.Injective f ∧ distortion W f = D}

/-- The binary main endpoint, for every sufficiently large cap (natural logarithms). -/
def BinaryLowerBound : Prop :=
  ∃ c : ℝ, 0 < c ∧ ∃ d₀ : ℕ, ∀ d : ℕ, d₀ ≤ d →
    ∃ n : ℕ, n ≤ d ∧ ∃ W : Finset (List Bool),
      2 ≤ W.card ∧ (∀ w ∈ W, w.length = n) ∧
      Real.exp (c * Real.sqrt (Real.log (d : ℝ) * Real.log (Real.log (d : ℝ)))) ≤ c1 W

theorem binary_torus : BinaryLowerBound := by
  sorry

end TreeEdit

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We give two independent constructions of binary words of one length at most <i>d</i> whose ordinary edit-distance metrics require <i>ℓ</i><sub>1</sub> distortion $`\exp(\Omega(\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d}))`$ for every sufficiently large <i>d</i>. We also prove a constant-distortion binary conversion for one prescribed input length. Together with the companion upper embedding theorem, these lower bounds determine the order of logarithmic distortion uniformly over finite alphabets with at least two symbols.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

An insertion or deletion changes the positions of every later symbol in a word. A long interval can therefore move at small edit cost even when many positions change. This tension between order and inexpensive shifts is central to the question studied here: how faithfully can one represent the edit distances of an entire set of words by distances in $\ell_1$?

We use ordinary edit distance $\operatorname{ED}(x,y)$: the minimum number of single-symbol insertions, deletions and substitutions taking $x$ to $y$, each with cost one. For a finite alphabet $\Sigma$ with at least two symbols and an integer $d\geq1$, let $\Sigma^{\leq d}$ be the set of words of length at most $d$, including the empty word. The cap constrains the words in the domain; an edit script computing their distance may pass through words of any finite length.

For a finite metric space $(X,\rho)$ with at least two points and an injective map $f:X\to\ell_1$, where $\ell_1$ is the space of absolutely summable real sequences, define $$\operatorname{dist}(f)=
 \left(\max_{x\ne y}\frac{\|f(x)-f(y)\|_1}{\rho(x,y)}\right)
 \left(\max_{x\ne y}\frac{\rho(x,y)}{\|f(x)-f(y)\|_1}\right).$$ Let $c_1(X,\rho)$ be the infimum of $\operatorname{dist}(f)$ over such maps, and put $E_\Sigma(d)=c_1(\Sigma^{\leq d},\operatorname{ED})$. No restriction is imposed on the dimension of the image or on the computation of the map. Throughout the paper, logarithms in asymptotic estimates are natural unless a base is written explicitly.

We prove the following lower bound in two independent ways. Each proof uses a tree to combine inexpensive shifts with a displacement that no alignment can hide.

<a id="stmt-thm-binary"></a>
<div id="thm:binary" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Binary lower bound). *There are an absolute constant $c>0$ and an integer $d_0$ such that, for every integer $d\geq d_0$, there exist an integer $1\leq n\leq d$ and a finite set $\mathcal W\subseteq\{0,1\}^n$ with $|\mathcal W|\geq2$ satisfying $$c_1(\mathcal W,\operatorname{ED})
 \geq \exp\!\left(c\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d}\right).$$*

</div>

The words in a witness all have the same length, although that length may depend on the cap $d$. At tree depth $k$, each construction produces logarithmic distortion of order at least $k\log k$ and logarithmic word length of order $k^2\log k$. Balancing these two scales gives Theorem <a href="#thm:binary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:binary">1</a> for every sufficiently large cap, not just for a subsequence of caps.

The lower bound is proved here without a companion input. The uniform upper embedding theorem of the finite-circle companion, stated precisely in Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">6</a>, then gives $$\log E_\Sigma(d)=\Theta\!\left(\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d}\right)$$ with constants independent of the finite alphabet $\Sigma$. This is a matching exponential scale, not a constant-factor estimate for $E_\Sigma(d)$ itself.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### History and the embedding question

The single-symbol error model is classical in coding and sequence comparison. Levenshtein studied binary codes correcting insertions and deletions <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Levenshtein1966](#ref-Levenshtein1966)], and Wagner and Fischer formulated string correction through increasing partial alignments <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[WagnerFischer1974](#ref-WagnerFischer1974), Sections 3 and 5]. An embedding asks for one set of coordinates that represents every pair in a domain. It is a different task from estimating the distance of one given pair of words. For supplied pairs of binary words, <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[OpenAIEditApprox2026](#ref-OpenAIEditApprox2026), Theorem 1.1] gives a randomized approximation scheme with almost-linear worst-case expected time for each fixed rational accuracy.

Ostrovsky and Rabani proved an $\ell_1$ distortion bound $\exp(O(\sqrt{\log n\,\log\log n}))$ for binary words of a fixed length $n$ <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[OstrovskyRabani2007](#ref-OstrovskyRabani2007)]. Their recursion compares overlapping substrings and represents their distributions in smaller metric spaces. They also noted extensions to larger alphabets and varying lengths. For the upper bound used at the end of this paper, the companion uniform upper embedding theorem <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[OpenAIUniformUpper2026](#ref-OpenAIUniformUpper2026), Uniform upper embedding theorem] gives the full quantitative statement on $\Sigma^{\leq d}$ with constants independent of the finite alphabet.

On the lower-bound side, Andoni, Deza, Gupta, Indyk and Raskhodnikova realized complete bipartite graph metrics in binary edit distance and obtained $\ell_1$ distortion approaching $3/2$ <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[AndoniDezaGuptaIndykRaskhodnikova2003](#ref-AndoniDezaGuptaIndykRaskhodnikova2003), Section 2 and Theorem 3.1]. Their witness uses three adjacent word lengths. Khot and Naor developed Fourier methods for nonembeddability and obtained an edit-distance lower bound of $(\log n)^{1/2-o(1)}$ for binary words of a fixed length <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[KhotNaor2006](#ref-KhotNaor2006)]. Their insertion–deletion metric and the ordinary metric used here differ by at most a factor of two. Krauthgamer and Rabani then proved an $\Omega(\log n)$ lower bound for binary words of length $n$ <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[KrauthgamerRabani2009](#ref-KrauthgamerRabani2009), Theorem 1.3]. Khot and Naor used Boolean noise sensitivity, while Krauthgamer and Rabani sharpened the obstruction through an influence inequality for bit flips and cyclic shifts. These cut and shift arguments are methodological predecessors of the proofs here. A cut records which pairs a binary coordinate separates, and Fourier coefficients describe how that separation changes under translations. We prove the particular translation estimates needed for our trees.

Alphabet reduction also has a substantial history. Schulman and Zuckerman use separation between long intervals of distinct codewords in concatenated insertion–deletion codes <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[SchulmanZuckerman1999](#ref-SchulmanZuckerman1999)], and synchronization strings protect positions against insertion and deletion errors <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[HaeuplerShahrasbi2017](#ref-HaeuplerShahrasbi2017)]. More directly, Bhattacharya, Dey, Goldenberg and Koucký state a map on all finite words with an insertion–deletion guarantee normalized by the sum of the word lengths, for pairs of positive total length. The block substitution used to prove their stated theorem has logarithmic width and, for a fixed error parameter, a constant target alphabet <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BhattacharyaDeyGoldenbergKoucky2024](#ref-BhattacharyaDeyGoldenbergKoucky2024), Theorem 3; full version, Section 3.2.1 and Lemma 3.3]. For equal-length inputs, their stated normalized guarantee, combined with the elementary delimiter construction below, gives a binary reduction with logarithmic width and absolute distortion. Our lower proofs do not use that result. They use the short delimiter code, whose width-dependent loss is harmless at the tree scale; the appendix gives a separate constant-distortion code for one prescribed word length. For comparison, the isometric Hamming-to-edit embedding of Bhattacharya et al. <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[BhattacharyaEtAl2026](#ref-BhattacharyaEtAl2026), Theorem 7] starts from a different input metric. The conversions proved here compare edit distance on the source words with edit distance on their binary encodings.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Two independent tree proofs

Both constructions use an ordered tree. A leaf has a state coordinate and produces a symbol carrying its leaf label. An internal node produces a long list of rows, each containing its child words in their fixed order. Advancing the row index deletes a few rows at one end and inserts a few at the other, so it is inexpensive. A distinguished second shift must instead separate the words even when an alignment crosses row boundaries.

The shared alignment lemma in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> handles this difficulty. Leaf labels preserve child indices, not row indices. An unsplit source row loses symbols because its child words are separated from the corresponding target words. A split source row forces a long unmatched target interval between consecutive matches. The intervals charged by different source rows are disjoint. Thus separation in every comparison of two rows implies separation of the whole words, without assuming that row boundaries match.

The two constructions arrange the shifts differently. In the finite tree of Section <a href="#sec:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite">3</a>, a child’s period is its parent’s period times a new prime. At an internal node, a vector $u_v$ advances rows and a multiple $w_v$ has the parent period. The distinct prime factors prevent a difference of row indices from cancelling the distinguished shift in too many children. This gives a deterministic separation proof on a finite group. For a Fourier character, activity at a node with few active children has a short period and is detected by an average of inexpensive row shifts. A character escaping every such test must be active on many leaves; individual leaf changes then bound its contribution. The section closes with binary witnesses for every large cap.

The circle tree in Sections <a href="#sec:torus-construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:torus-construction">4</a> and <a href="#sec:torus-fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:torus-fourier">5</a> uses quantized phases instead of finite residues. Random prime lists and coefficients make the root half-turn separate every base phase. The probabilistic assertion is only for one fixed shift; the all-phase conclusion follows deterministically from the alignment lemma. In its Fourier argument, tiny rotations detect large integer frequency sums and rational row shifts detect failed prime congruences. When both tests fail, divisibility forces every nonzero sum to continue through many children. This supplies the second binary proof without using the finite tree.

Both routes use the same elementary delimiter code from Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>. Its distortion loss grows with the block width, but that width is only $O(k\log k)$ here and does not change the logarithmic lower-bound scale. Appendix <a href="#sec:code" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:code">7</a> proves a stronger, separate alphabet-conversion theorem: for words of one prescribed length $M$ over at most $M$ letters, blocks of width $M^2$ preserve edit distance up to scale and an absolute factor $804$. Its proof is independent of both trees, and neither headline lower bound requires this stronger conversion.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Edit deficits, ordered rows and binary transfer

Both trees need four elementary tools. Subsequence deficits and an alignment lemma turn separation of child blocks into separation of whole words, even when matches cross row boundaries. A cut representation permits the later Fourier estimates, and a prime count supplies the periods. Finally, a short binary code transfers either tree’s obstruction to binary words with a controlled width loss.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Subsequence matchings

<a id="eq:deficit"></a>
A common subsequence matching of words $x=x_1\cdots x_a$ and $y=y_1\cdots y_b$ is a list of pairs $(i_1,j_1),\ldots,(i_s,j_s)$ whose two index lists are strictly increasing and for which $x_{i_t}=y_{j_t}$ for every $t$. Its largest possible size is $\operatorname{LCS}(x,y)$. The insertion–deletion distance is $$\Delta(x,y)=|x|+|y|-2\operatorname{LCS}(x,y).$$ Indeed, symbols of $x$ that survive an insertion–deletion script form a common subsequence; conversely, retaining a longest common subsequence and deleting and inserting the remaining symbols attains this value. Replacing each substitution by one deletion and one insertion gives <a id="eq:metric-conversion"></a>$$\label{eq:metric-conversion}
 \operatorname{ED}(x,y)\leq\Delta(x,y)\leq2\operatorname{ED}(x,y).$$ For words of equal length $n$, put $r_n(x,y)=n-\operatorname{LCS}(x,y)$. Thus $$\label{eq:deficit}
 r_n(x,y)\leq\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\leq2r_n(x,y).$$ A matching of size $n-E$ between two length-$n$ words leaves exactly $E$ unmatched positions on each side.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The common alignment lemma

Suppose each row consists of one block from each of several pairwise disjoint alphabets, in the same order. An equal-symbol match must then join blocks with the same index, although their row indices can differ. Products of words in the next lemma mean concatenation in increasing index order.

<a id="stmt-lem-tagged"></a>
<div id="lem:tagged" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:local-tagged"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Rows with ordered tags). *Let $T,B\geq1$ and $m\geq2$ be integers, and let $\Gamma_1,\ldots,\Gamma_m$ be pairwise disjoint alphabets. For $1\leq a,b\leq T$ and $1\leq i\leq m$, take $X_{a,i},Y_{b,i}\in\Gamma_i^B$, and define $$X=\prod_{a=1}^T(X_{a,1}\cdots X_{a,m}),\qquad
 Y=\prod_{b=1}^T(Y_{b,1}\cdots Y_{b,m}),\qquad N=TmB.$$ Suppose $d\geq0$ is real and $$\label{eq:local-tagged}
 \sum_{i=1}^m r_B(X_{a,i},Y_{b,i})\geq d
 \qquad(1\leq a,b\leq T).$$ With $g=(m-1)B$, one has <a id="eq:tagged-result"></a>$$\label{eq:tagged-result}
 r_N(X,Y)\geq\frac{Tdg}{d+g}
 \geq\frac T2\min(d,g).$$ In particular, $d\leq g$ implies $r_N(X,Y)\geq Td/2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tagged"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tagged</summary>

*Proof.* Fix an increasing matching and let $E$ be its unmatched count on either side. Call a source row split if it has matches in at least two target rows, and let $s$ be the number of split source rows.

<a id="eq:tagged-unsplit"></a>
A source row that is not split and has matches sends them all into one target row, say $b$. The disjoint alphabets confine matches from its block $i$ to block $i$ in that target row. The restriction of the matching to those two blocks has size at most their $\operatorname{LCS}$, even if other source rows also use the target block. Condition <a href="#eq:local-tagged" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-tagged">[eq:local-tagged]</a> therefore leaves at least $d$ unmatched source positions in this row. A source row with no matches loses all $mB$ positions, again at least $d$, since <a href="#eq:local-tagged" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-tagged">[eq:local-tagged]</a> implies $d\leq mB$. The nonsplit rows are disjoint, so $$\label{eq:tagged-unsplit}
 E\geq d(T-s).$$

<a id="eq:tagged-split"></a>
For a split source row, list its matches in order and choose two consecutive entries at a transition between target rows. They are also consecutive in the entire matching: every source position between them lies in the same source row, so an intervening global match would have appeared in its list. Let $i,i'$ be their source block indices. Their order gives $i\leq i'$, and disjoint alphabets give the same two block indices in the target. Between their target positions lie at least $$(m-i+i'-1)B\geq(m-1)B=g$$ positions in complete blocks. This is the count for adjacent target rows; intervening rows only increase it. All positions in that open target interval are unmatched, because the selected matches are globally consecutive. The intervals selected for different source rows are disjoint by strict monotonicity of the matching. Hence $$\label{eq:tagged-split}
 E\geq sg.$$

Since $g>0$, <a href="#eq:tagged-split" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tagged-split">[eq:tagged-split]</a> gives $s\leq E/g$. Substitute this in <a href="#eq:tagged-unsplit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tagged-unsplit">[eq:tagged-unsplit]</a> to obtain $E\geq d(T-E/g)$, and rearrange: $E\geq Tdg/(d+g)$. If $d\leq g$, then $dg/(d+g)\geq d/2$; if $g\leq d$, it is at least $g/2$. These bounds hold for every matching, including when $d=0$ or $T=1$, and therefore for a longest one. ◻

</details>

The two inequalities in the proof bound the same number $E$ using different words: nonsplit rows give source losses, and split rows give target losses. Equal total lengths make the unmatched counts equal. The fixed order, equal block lengths and disjoint tags supply the target gaps; no alignment of row boundaries is assumed. Figure <a href="#fig:tagged-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:tagged-gap">1</a> illustrates the gap created by a split row.

<figure id="fig:tagged-gap">

<figcaption>A split source row in Lemma <a href="#lem:tagged" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tagged">2</a>, with $m = 5$. The two arrows are consecutive matches from source blocks $i = 2$ and $i′ = 4$. Their target positions lie in different rows, so all positions between them are unmatched. The six shaded complete blocks contribute $(m−i+i′−1)B = 6B ≥ (m−1)B$ positions. The diagram is schematic; target rows further apart would only enlarge the gap.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-tagged-gap"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:tagged-gap`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.98cm,y=0.85cm,>=Latex,
 every node/.style={font=\small}]
 \foreach \block in {1,...,5} {
  \draw ({\block+1.5},2) rectangle ({\block+2.5},2.6);
  \node at ({\block+2},2.3) {$\block$};
 }
 \node[anchor=east] at (2.3,2.3) {source row $a$};
 \foreach \block in {1,...,5} {
  \draw ({\block-1},0) rectangle (\block,0.6);
  \draw ({\block+4},0) rectangle ({\block+5},0.6);
 }
 \foreach \block in {3,4,5} {
  \fill[blue!15] ({\block-1},0) rectangle (\block,0.6);
 }
 \foreach \block in {1,2,3} {
  \fill[blue!15] ({\block+4},0) rectangle ({\block+5},0.6);
 }
 \foreach \block in {1,...,5} {
  \node at ({\block-0.5},0.3) {$\block$};
  \node at ({\block+4.5},0.3) {$\block$};
 }
 \draw[very thick] (5,0) -- (5,0.6);
 \node at (2.5,-0.4) {target row $b$};
 \node at (7.5,-0.4) {target row $b+1$};
 \draw[blue,thick,->] (4,2) -- (1.5,0.6);
 \draw[blue,thick,->] (6,2) -- (8.5,0.6);
 \fill[blue] (4,2) circle (1.5pt);
 \fill[blue] (6,2) circle (1.5pt);
 \draw[blue,<->] (1.5,0.95) -- (8.5,0.95);
```

```text
- Bottom: two consecutive rows placed side by side:

  - Right: labeled "target row $b + 1$", containing blocks numbered 1 to 5, where blocks 1, 2, 3 are shaded blue, and blocks 4 and 5 are unshaded.
- Arrows: two blue directed arrows point from the source row to target rows:

  - Another arrow from block 4 of source row $a$ to block 4 of target row $b + 1$.
- Dimension/bracket line: above the shaded blocks between the two arrow targets, a horizontal dimension bracket spans from block 2 of target row $b$ to block 4 of target row $b + 1$, labeled "unmatched target gap".
```
